## Referência: references/squad/data/story-mappings.yaml

```yaml
# Story Mappings — Canonical Vocabulary
# Translation tables for refining vague narrative requests into professional storytelling terminology.
# All storytelling squad agents should consult this file before generating output.
#
# Based on: Stitch design-mappings pattern (google-labs-code/stitch-skills)
# Extended with: Campbell, Duarte, StoryBrand, Story Grid, ABT, Ganz
#
# Epic: epic-cross-squad-prompt-consistency (Story 5)
# Version: 1.0.0

metadata:
  version: "1.0.0"
  squad: "storytelling"
  created: "2026-03-23"

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# TABLE 1: Narrative Terms
# Translates vague story requests into professional narrative terminology.
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
narrative_terms:
  - vague: "conta uma historia"
    enhanced: "structured narrative with hook, rising action, climax, and resolution tied to a clear lesson"
  - vague: "tell a story"
    enhanced: "structured narrative with hook, rising action, climax, and resolution tied to a clear lesson"
  - vague: "apresentacao"
    enhanced: "persuasive presentation with sparkline structure (what is vs. what could be) and clear call to action"
  - vague: "pitch"
    enhanced: "high-stakes pitch with frame control, status alignment, and time-constrained narrative arc"
  - vague: "discurso"
    enhanced: "leadership speech with story of self, story of us, and story of now (Ganz public narrative)"
  - vague: "roteiro"
    enhanced: "screenplay structure with 15 beats (Save the Cat), clear genre conventions, and emotional turning points"
  - vague: "jornada do heroi"
    enhanced: "17-stage monomyth with departure, initiation, and return arcs (Campbell hero's journey)"
  - vague: "brand story"
    enhanced: "StoryBrand 7-part framework: character, problem (3 levels), guide, plan, call to action, failure, success"
  - vague: "elevator pitch"
    enhanced: "ABT structure: [situation] AND [complication] BUT [resolution] in 30 seconds or less"
  - vague: "começo"
    enhanced: "opening hook: 5-second moment that creates curiosity gap or emotional entry point"
  - vague: "conflito"
    enhanced: "rising tension through progressive complications, stakes escalation, and obstacle mounting"
  - vague: "final"
    enhanced: "resolution with transformation reveal, lesson crystallization, and callback to opening"
  - vague: "personagem"
    enhanced: "character with clear want (external), need (internal), wound (backstory), and transformation arc"
  - vague: "mensagem"
    enhanced: "controlling idea: one-sentence theme that the story proves through its climax"
  - vague: "engajamento"
    enhanced: "audience engagement through status transactions, pattern interrupts, and participatory moments"
  - vague: "improviso"
    enhanced: "spontaneous narrative using 'yes and' principle, status play, and Johnstone's improv techniques"
  - vague: "storytelling de dados"
    enhanced: "data narrative with SlideDocs structure: context, insight, implication, action"
  - vague: "caso de sucesso"
    enhanced: "customer success story: situation (before), struggle (obstacle), solution (turning point), success (measurable result)"

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# TABLE 2: Framework Selector
# Maps objectives to the recommended storytelling framework.
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
framework_selector:
  - objective: "vender produto/servico"
    framework: "StoryBrand (Donald Miller)"
    agent: "donald-miller"
    reason: "Positions the customer as the hero, brand as the guide. Clear CTA structure."

  - objective: "pitch de investimento"
    framework: "Pitch Anything (Oren Klaff)"
    agent: "oren-klaff"
    reason: "Frame control, status alignment, and time-constrained narrative for high-stakes pitches."

  - objective: "apresentacao executiva"
    framework: "Resonate (Nancy Duarte)"
    agent: "nancy-duarte"
    reason: "Sparkline structure (what is vs. what could be) for persuasive business presentations."

  - objective: "mobilizar equipe/comunidade"
    framework: "Public Narrative (Marshall Ganz)"
    agent: "marshall-ganz"
    reason: "Story of Self, Story of Us, Story of Now for leadership and movement building."

  - objective: "historia pessoal/keynote"
    framework: "Storyworthy (Matthew Dicks)"
    agent: "matthew-dicks"
    reason: "5-second moment technique for finding and crafting compelling personal stories."

  - objective: "roteiro de video/serie"
    framework: "Save the Cat (Blake Snyder)"
    agent: "blake-snyder"
    reason: "15 beats structure for commercially viable screenplays and video content."

  - objective: "diagnostico de estrutura narrativa"
    framework: "Story Grid (Shawn Coyne)"
    agent: "shawn-coyne"
    reason: "Analytical framework for diagnosing structural problems in existing narratives."

  - objective: "comunicacao rapida (30s-2min)"
    framework: "ABT (Park Howell)"
    agent: "park-howell"
    reason: "And-But-Therefore structure for concise, impactful business communication."

  - objective: "conteudo episodico/serie"
    framework: "Story Circle (Dan Harmon)"
    agent: "dan-harmon"
    reason: "8-step circular structure for episodic content and recurring narratives."

  - objective: "jornada epica/transformacao profunda"
    framework: "Hero's Journey (Joseph Campbell)"
    agent: "joseph-campbell"
    reason: "17-stage monomyth for deep transformation narratives and brand mythologies."

  - objective: "desbloqueio criativo"
    framework: "Impro (Keith Johnstone)"
    agent: "keith-johnstone"
    reason: "Status transactions and spontaneity for unlocking creative blocks."

  - objective: "storytelling de negocio/marca"
    framework: "Stories That Stick (Kindra Hall)"
    agent: "kindra-hall"
    reason: "4 story types (value, founder, purpose, customer) for strategic business storytelling."

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# TABLE 3: Story Elements
# Translates generic narrative elements into specific, actionable components.
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
story_elements:
  hook:
    - type: "curiosity gap"
      description: "Open a question the audience MUST have answered"
      example: "The day I almost lost everything, I learned the one thing that actually matters."
    - type: "in medias res"
      description: "Start in the middle of the action"
      example: "The phone rang at 3am. I already knew what it meant."
    - type: "provocative statement"
      description: "Challenge a belief the audience holds"
      example: "Everything you know about leadership is wrong."
    - type: "5-second moment"
      description: "Start at the exact moment of transformation (Dicks technique)"
      example: "I was standing in the parking lot when I realized I would never go back."
    - type: "status transaction"
      description: "Establish status dynamic between characters (Johnstone)"
      example: "He looked at me like I was the help. I was the CEO."

  structure:
    - type: "3-act"
      description: "Setup, Confrontation, Resolution"
    - type: "5-act"
      description: "Exposition, Rising Action, Climax, Falling Action, Denouement"
    - type: "sparkline"
      description: "Alternating 'what is' and 'what could be' (Duarte)"
    - type: "ABT"
      description: "And (agreement), But (contradiction), Therefore (consequence)"
    - type: "story circle"
      description: "You, Need, Go, Search, Find, Take, Return, Change (Harmon)"

  climax:
    - type: "revelation"
      description: "Hidden truth is revealed that reframes everything"
    - type: "choice"
      description: "Character must choose between two irreconcilable values"
    - type: "sacrifice"
      description: "Character gives up something valued to gain something greater"
    - type: "transformation"
      description: "Character becomes fundamentally different (identity shift)"

# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
# TABLE 4: Audience Context
# Maps audience type to storytelling approach.
# ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
audience_context:
  - audience: "investors"
    approach: "Lead with traction, frame problem as market opportunity, end with vision and ask"
    avoid: "Emotional stories without data backing, long personal narratives"
    framework_hint: "oren-klaff or nancy-duarte"

  - audience: "customers"
    approach: "Position them as hero, show transformation through social proof, clear CTA"
    avoid: "Company-centric language, features without benefits"
    framework_hint: "donald-miller or kindra-hall"

  - audience: "employees/team"
    approach: "Story of us, shared values, collective challenge, agency and urgency"
    avoid: "Top-down directives, empty inspiration without action steps"
    framework_hint: "marshall-ganz or nancy-duarte"

  - audience: "conference/keynote"
    approach: "Personal vulnerability, universal lesson, memorable 5-second moment"
    avoid: "Corporate jargon, death by slides, no personal stake"
    framework_hint: "matthew-dicks or nancy-duarte"

  - audience: "social media"
    approach: "Hook in first 3 seconds, ABT structure, end with shareable insight"
    avoid: "Long setup, burying the hook, no visual anchor"
    framework_hint: "park-howell or dan-harmon"

  - audience: "students/learners"
    approach: "Discovery-based narrative, Socratic questioning, progressive revelation"
    avoid: "Lecturing, telling without showing, no participation"
    framework_hint: "keith-johnstone or joseph-campbell"
```


## Referência: references/squad/research/blake-snyder-save-the-cat-research.md

# Blake Snyder Save the Cat! Research Document

## Document Metadata

```yaml
research:
  subject: "Blake Snyder - Save the Cat! Methodology"
  domain: "Screenwriting Structure"
  purpose: "Deep research for agent creation"
  created: "2026-01-30"
  word_count: "500+ lines"
  sources: "Primary and secondary sources"
```

---

## Executive Summary

Blake Snyder (October 3, 1957 - August 4, 2009) was an American screenwriter, consultant, author, and educator who created one of the most influential screenwriting methodologies in Hollywood history. His "Save the Cat!" system provides a precise 15-beat structure with exact timing percentages that has become the de facto standard for commercial storytelling in film, television, and increasingly, novels.

This research document consolidates all key frameworks, principles, and methodologies from Snyder's three major works:
1. "Save the Cat! The Last Book on Screenwriting You'll Ever Need" (2005) - PRIMARY
2. "Save the Cat! Goes to the Movies" (2007) - Genre Applications
3. "Save the Cat! Strikes Back" (2009) - Advanced Techniques

---

## Part 1: Biography and Professional Background

### Early Life and Family

Blake Snyder was born October 3, 1957, and grew up in a creative household. His father, Kenneth C. T. Snyder, was an Emmy-winning TV producer of children's shows in the 1960s and 1970s, including:
- The Funny Company
- Hot Wheels
- Animated segments on Sesame Street
- Big Blue Marble
- Roger Ramjet (cult classic)

This early exposure to story structure and entertainment production deeply influenced Blake's understanding of what makes stories work commercially.

### Education

Snyder earned a B.A. degree in English from Georgetown University, then returned to Los Angeles to begin his entertainment career.

### Screenwriting Career

**Early Work:**
- Began writing for Disney TV series "Kids Incorporated"
- Started full-time screenwriting in 1987
- Member of the Writers Guild of America for over 20 years

**Major Script Sales:**
- "Stop! Or My Mom Will Shoot" (1989) - First spec sale, $500,000 in bidding war
- "Blank Check" (1994) - Million-dollar sale, co-written with Colby Carr for Disney
- "Nuclear Family" - Million-dollar sale to Steven Spielberg
- Sold dozens of scripts in his 20-year career

**Films Produced:**
- "Stop! Or My Mom Will Shoot" (1992) - Starring Sylvester Stallone
- "Blank Check" (1994) - Disney hit

Snyder was named "Hollywood's most successful spec screenwriter."

### Teaching Career

Snyder taught screenwriting at:
- Chapman University
- UCLA
- Vanderbilt University
- Beijing Film Academy

His books became the basis of screenwriting classes at major universities worldwide.

### Death and Legacy

Blake Snyder died unexpectedly on August 4, 2009, from either a pulmonary embolism or cardiac arrest. The DreamWorks animated film "How to Train Your Dragon" (2010) was dedicated to him.

His book "Save the Cat!" remained the #1 selling screenwriting manual on Amazon, with 34+ printings by 2018.

**Sources:**
- [Wikipedia - Blake Snyder](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blake_Snyder)
- [Save the Cat - About Blake Snyder](https://savethecat.com/bio)
- [IMDb - Blake Snyder](https://www.imdb.com/name/nm0811414/)
- [Writer's Digest](https://www.writersdigest.com/script-notes/blake-snyder-was-the-master-of-structure)

---

## Part 2: The 15-Beat Structure (BS2)

### Overview

The Blake Snyder Beat Sheet (BS2) is the core framework of the Save the Cat! methodology. It divides the three-act structure into 15 specific beats with exact page numbers for a 110-page screenplay (where 1 page = 1 minute of screen time).

### The Complete 15 Beats with Timing

#### ACT ONE (Thesis) - Pages 1-25 (25%)

**1. Opening Image (Page 1) - 1%**
- Single scene beat
- Shows "before" snapshot of protagonist and their flawed world
- Sets tone, mood, type, and scope of the story
- Should mirror the Final Image to show transformation
- Example: In "The Matrix," a blinking command line cursor
- Example: In "Interstellar," a replica NASA space shuttle gathering dust

**2. Theme Stated (Page 5) - 5%**
- Secondary character states the theme in an offhand manner
- The protagonist doesn't understand it yet
- Plants the seed of what the movie is really about
- Example: In "The Matrix," Neo is told he needs to "unplug"
- Usually subtle, easily missed on first viewing

**3. Set-Up (Pages 1-10) - 1-10%**
- Multi-scene beat introducing the protagonist's ordinary world
- All major A-story characters introduced or hinted at
- Must present the "Six Things That Need Fixing":
  - Character flaws
  - Basic needs
  - Wishes
  - Enemies and rivals
  - Things missing from hero's life
- These will be "paid off" later in the story

**4. Catalyst (Page 12) - 10-12%**
- Single scene beat - the inciting incident
- Something happens that disrupts the hero's status quo
- Life-changing moment that sets story in motion
- The protagonist's world is flipped upside-down
- Nothing will ever be the same
- Example: In "The Hunger Games," Prim's name is drawn at the Reaping

**5. Debate (Pages 12-25) - 12-25%**
- Multi-scene beat where hero questions whether to accept the challenge
- Presented as a question: "Should I go?" "What do I do?"
- Shows hero's reluctance to change
- The hero may seek advice, gather information, resist the call
- Last chance to show the "old world" before transformation begins

**6. Break into Two (Page 25) - 25%**
- Single scene beat marking the end of Act One
- The protagonist makes a decisive choice
- Leaves their comfort zone and enters the "upside-down world" of Act Two
- Often involves a literal change in geography
- Example: Frodo leaving the Shire in "Lord of the Rings"
- Example: Neo discovering reality in "The Matrix"
- Example: Andy accepting the Runway job in "The Devil Wears Prada"

#### ACT TWO (Antithesis) - Pages 25-85 (50%)

**7. B Story (Page 30) - 30%**
- Single scene beat introducing the subplot
- Usually a love story, friendship, or mentorship
- Carries the theme of the movie
- The B Story character helps the protagonist learn the lesson
- A-Story = what hero WANTS (tangible goal)
- B-Story = what hero NEEDS (spiritual goal)
- Runs parallel to main story throughout Act Two

**8. Fun and Games (Pages 30-55) - 30-55%**
- Multi-scene beat - the "promise of the premise"
- What audiences came to see
- The trailer moments, poster images
- Hero either shining or floundering in new world
- Not required to be "fun" - means delivering on your hook
- Example: In "The Matrix," Neo learns martial arts in The Construct
- Every scene still needs conflict

**9. Midpoint (Page 55) - 50%**
- Single scene beat at the exact center of the story
- Either a "False Victory" or "False Defeat"
- Stakes are raised significantly
- Often introduces a ticking clock
- The ground shifts beneath the protagonist
- Changes the game for the second half
- Connected inversely to All Is Lost beat
- Example: In "Jurassic Park," the T. rex escapes

**10. Bad Guys Close In (Pages 55-75) - 55-75%**
- Multi-scene beat where external and internal pressures mount
- Antagonist forces regroup and attack
- Internal doubts, flaws, and jealousies disintegrate the team
- If Midpoint was false victory, things start going wrong
- If Midpoint was false defeat, a temporary rally occurs
- Things progressively get worse

**11. All Is Lost (Page 75) - 75%**
- Single scene beat - the lowest point of the story
- Hero hits rock bottom with no hope
- Contains the "Whiff of Death" - something dies (literally or metaphorically)
- Could be: death of mentor, collapse of plan, broken belief, loss of innocence
- Inverse of the Midpoint: it's never as bad as it seems here
- The old world, old character, old way of thinking dies
- Example: In "Miss Congeniality," Sandra faces losing her badge and identity

**12. Dark Night of the Soul (Pages 75-85) - 75-85%**
- Multi-scene beat - the darkness before the dawn
- Hero wallows in absolute hopelessness
- Processing the All Is Lost beat
- Unlike the Catalyst (happened TO hero), All Is Lost was caused BY hero
- Questions reflect on how they ended up here
- Realization forces them to see the need for change
- This time, ready to embrace it

**13. Break into Three (Page 85) - 85%**
- Single scene beat - the epiphany moment
- A and B stories intertwine
- Hero finds the solution using what they've learned
- Understands what they have to do to fix their problem
- The final act (Synthesis) begins

#### ACT THREE (Synthesis) - Pages 85-110 (25%)

**14. Finale (Pages 85-110) - 85-99%**
- Multi-scene beat - the final showdown
- Hero applies the lessons learned
- The old world turns over, new world is created
- Bad guys dealt with in ascending order
- Five-Point Finale structure (from "Strikes Back"):
  1. Gathering the Team
  2. Executing the Plan
  3. High Tower Surprise
  4. Dig Deep Down
  5. Execution of New Plan
- Not enough for hero to succeed - they must change the world

**15. Final Image (Page 110) - 100%**
- Single scene beat
- Shows "after" snapshot of protagonist
- Mirrors the Opening Image to show transformation
- Demonstrates how much has changed
- Proof that the journey was worth it

**Sources:**
- [Wikipedia - Save the Cat!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Save_the_Cat!:_The_Last_Book_on_Screenwriting_You'll_Ever_Need)
- [StudioBinder Beat Sheet Explained](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/save-the-cat-beat-sheet/)
- [Kindlepreneur Beat Sheet Guide](https://kindlepreneur.com/save-the-cat-beat-sheet/)
- [Indie Cinema Academy - Chapter Four Summary](https://indiecinemaacademy.com/save-the-cat-screenwriting-book-chapter-four-summary/)

---

## Part 3: The 10 Story Genres

### Philosophy Behind the Genres

Snyder argued that traditional genre labels (Comedy, Drama, Thriller) don't tell you much about story structure. His 10 genres describe HOW stories work, not just what TYPE they are.

### The Complete 10 Genres

#### 1. Monster in the House (MITH)

**Definition:** A protagonist is trapped with a monster in a defined setting.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. A Monster - supernatural or human, embodies evil
2. A House - confined space (could be a town, ship, etc.)
3. A Sin - someone's transgression invites the monster

**Key Insight:** The monster is typically a metaphor for consequences brought by sin. Only after the sin is acknowledged and atoned for can the monster be destroyed.

**Examples:**
- Alien
- Fatal Attraction
- Scream
- The Ring
- Saw
- Jaws (the "house" is Amity Island)

#### 2. Golden Fleece

**Definition:** A journey or quest story where the hero must obtain a prize.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. A Road - spanning oceans, time, or across the street - demarcates growth
2. A Team - people who represent what the hero lacks
3. A Prize - time-sensitive goal to achieve

**Key Insight:** The joy is in the journey, not the destination. Often includes a "Road Apple" that stops the trip cold.

**Examples:**
- Bad News Bears
- Planes, Trains and Automobiles
- Saving Private Ryan
- Ocean's Eleven
- Maria Full of Grace

#### 3. Out of the Bottle

**Definition:** A character is granted a wish, but the wish goes wrong.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. A Wish - or curse that changes reality
2. A Spell - the rules of the magic
3. A Lesson - what must be learned to break the spell

**Key Insight:** The key elements are comedy and irony. The wish reveals what the character really needs.

**Examples:**
- Freaky Friday
- Cocoon
- The Nutty Professor
- What Women Want
- Eternal Sunshine of the Spotless Mind

#### 4. Dude with a Problem

**Definition:** An innocent hero is suddenly thrust into a life-or-death battle.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. An Innocent Hero - ordinary person, not equipped for this
2. A Sudden Event - no time to prepare
3. A Life-or-Death Situation - survival stakes

**Key Insight:** The "dude" is everyPerson - we identify because we'd be equally unprepared.

**Examples:**
- Die Hard
- The Martian
- Home Alone
- Three Days of the Condor
- Sleeping with the Enemy
- Open Water

#### 5. Rites of Passage

**Definition:** A story centered around internal conflict and life transitions.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. A Life Problem - affliction, challenge, or passage
2. Wrong Approach - protagonist handles it badly at first
3. Acceptance - finally learns to deal with the problem

**Key Insight:** The protagonist spends much of the story dealing with the problem in counterproductive ways that leave them weaker.

**Examples:**
- 10
- Ordinary People
- Days of Wine and Roses
- 28 Days
- Napoleon Dynamite

#### 6. Buddy Love

**Definition:** Two characters who start out hating each other must work together.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. An Incomplete Hero - missing something
2. A Counterpart - has what hero lacks (often opposites)
3. A Complication - something threatens the relationship

**Key Insight:** The relationship is the plot. Not just romantic - can be friendship, professional partnership.

**Examples:**
- Lethal Weapon (buddy cop)
- Rain Man
- When Harry Met Sally
- E.T. (boy and alien)
- Brokeback Mountain

#### 7. Whydunit

**Definition:** A detective uncovers evil that ultimately turns out to be... "us."

**Three Required Elements:**
1. A Detective - may be amateur or professional
2. A Secret - about human nature
3. A Dark Turn - the "why" shocks us about humanity

**Key Insight:** Unlike Golden Fleece, the hero doesn't change - WE (the audience) discover something about human nature we didn't think was possible.

**Examples:**
- All the President's Men
- Blade Runner
- Fargo
- Mystic The Keymaker
- Brick
- Chinatown

#### 8. Fool Triumphant

**Definition:** An underdog who is unaware of any greatness they possess enters an establishment and causes conflict.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. A Fool - innocence is their strength, easily ignored
2. An Establishment - the group the fool comes up against
3. A Transmutation - fool becomes someone new (often with a "name change")

**Key Insight:** The world tries to destroy the fool, but they narrowly escape each attempt through luck. Eventually, the fool mutates into a hero.

**Examples:**
- Being There
- Tootsie
- Forrest Gump
- Legally Blonde
- The 40-Year-Old Virgin

#### 9. Institutionalized

**Definition:** Explores power dynamics between individuals and groups.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. A Group - family, organization, institution
2. A Choice - conform or rebel
3. A Sacrifice - what must be given up

**Key Insight:** Asks: who matters most, the individual or the group? Often the protagonist realizes the group is bad and tries to expose them.

**Examples:**
- M*A*S*H
- Do the Right Thing
- Office Space
- Training Day
- Crash
- One Flew Over the Cuckoo's Nest

#### 10. Superhero

**Definition:** An extraordinary person faces the ordinary world.

**Three Required Elements:**
1. A Special Power - what makes them extraordinary
2. A Nemesis - equal and opposite
3. A Curse - the downside of being special

**Key Insight:** Exact opposite of Dude with a Problem. The Superhero is extraordinary put into extraordinary situations only they can resolve.

**Examples:**
- Raging Bull
- The Lion King
- The Matrix
- Gladiator
- Spider-Man 2
- Batman

**Sources:**
- [Save the Cat - Get Started](https://savethecat.com/get-started)
- [The Independent Initiative - 10 Genres](https://theindependentinitiative.com/2020/03/17/every-movie-ever-fits-into-these-10-genres-from-save-the-cat/)
- [Scene One - Save the Cat Genres](https://sceneone.app/blog/the-ten-save-the-cat-story-genres/)
- [Freshmen Screenplay - 10 Types](https://freshmenscreenplay.com/the-save-the-cat-10-types-of-movies/)

---

## Part 4: The Board Method

### Overview

"The Board" is Snyder's visual story planning technique using index cards on a corkboard.

### The 40-Card Structure

**Layout:**
- Four rows, 10 cards per row = 40 cards total
- Represents the average number of beats in a movie

**Row Distribution:**
1. **Row 1 (Act One):** Pages 1-25
2. **Row 2 (Act Two-A):** Pages 25-55 (up to Midpoint)
3. **Row 3 (Act Two-B):** Pages 55-85 (up to Act Three)
4. **Row 4 (Act Three):** Pages 85-110

### What Goes on Each Card

Each index card represents a scene and must include:

**1. Scene Description**
- Brief summary of what happens
- Clear beginning, middle, end (mini-movie)

**2. Conflict Symbol (><)**
- Who is in opposition to whom
- Every scene needs clear conflict

**3. Emotional Change (+/-)**
- How does the scene start emotionally?
- How does it end?
- Must have an emotional shift from start to finish

### Color-Coding System

Snyder recommends color-coding cards based on:
- **Characters** - different colors for different POV characters
- **Storylines** - A-story vs B-story
- **Themes** - visual tracking of theme threads

### Purpose of the Board

The Board allows you to:
- "See" your story before writing
- Test different scenes and story arcs
- Work out kinks before committing to pages
- Easily rearrange story elements
- Identify pacing problems visually

**Sources:**
- [Save the Cat - Get Started](https://savethecat.com/get-started)
- [Wikipedia - Save the Cat!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Save_the_Cat!:_The_Last_Book_on_Screenwriting_You'll_Ever_Need)
- [StudioBinder](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/save-the-cat-beat-sheet/)

---

## Part 5: The Logline Formula

### Why Loglines Matter

Snyder argued you should write the logline and title BEFORE writing the story. If you can't pitch it in one sentence, you don't have a movie.

### The Four Requirements of a Good Logline

**1. Irony**
- The most important element
- "The hook" that catches interest
- Highlights struggle and conflict
- Incites the question: "How is THAT going to work?"
- Example: "A businessman falls in love with a hooker he hires to be his date for the weekend" (Pretty Woman)

**2. A Compelling Mental Picture**
- Must imply the entire movie
- Reader should see the whole story unfold
- Tells something about setting and/or timeframe
- Example: "She's the perfect woman—until she has a drink" (Blind Date)

**3. Audience and Cost**
- Demarcates tone and target audience
- Gives sense of budget/scope
- Buyers know if it can make a profit

**4. A Killer Title**
- One-two punch with the logline
- "Says what it is" in a clever way
- Memorable and evocative

### The Logline Formula

**Template:**
"A(n) [ADJECTIVE] [HERO TYPE] must [GOAL] when [SITUATION], but [COMPLICATION]."

**Snyder's Components:**
- An adjective to describe your hero/heroine
- An adjective for the villain
- A compelling goal we identify with as human beings

**Examples:**
- Pretty Woman: "A businessman falls in love with a hooker he hires to be his date for the weekend."
- Blind Date: "She's the perfect woman—until she has a drink."
- Die Hard: "A New York cop tries to save his wife and others taken hostage by terrorists during a Christmas party at her company."

**Sources:**
- [Indie Cinema Academy - Chapter One Summary](https://indiecinemaacademy.com/save-the-cat-screenwriting-book-chapter-one-summary/)
- [Lisa Cooper Ellison - Logline Ingredients](https://lisacooperellison.com/do-the-words-logline-or-elevator-pitch-make-you-cringe-use-blake-snyders-three-ingredients-to-create-a-winning-pitch/)
- [DIYMFA - Pitching with Save the Cat](https://diymfa.com/community/pitching-help-save-the-cat/)

---

## Part 6: Immutable Laws of Screenplay Physics

### Overview

Chapter Six of "Save the Cat!" presents common sense rules based on experience in Hollywood trenches.

### Save the Cat (The Rule)

**Definition:** The hero must do something when we meet them so that we like them and want them to win.

**Key Insight:** Even an anti-hero needs to be likeable. Give them a moment early that shows their humanity.

**Example:** In Disney's Aladdin, Aladdin shares his stolen food with hungry kids.

### Pope in the Pool

**Definition:** A distracting way to bury exposition.

**Origin:** A friend's script had Vatican officials deliver boring exposition while the pope swims in a special Vatican pool. The unusual visual distracts from the information dump.

**Application:** If you have a lot of backstory to tell, divert the audience's attention while doing so.

### Double Mumbo Jumbo

**Definition:** The tendency to pile on multiple forms of "magic" in a story.

**The Rule:** Audiences will suspend disbelief ONCE. You cannot ask them to believe in aliens AND vampires in the same world.

**Example:** Spider-Man - being bitten by a spider that gives powers is okay. But across town a lab accident giving Green Goblin powers too? That's Double Mumbo Jumbo.

**Application:** Pick one form of magic/fantasy and stick with it.

### Laying Pipe

**Definition:** Snyder's catch-all term for setup and exposition.

**The Warning:** Beware laying too much pipe before the story gets going.

**Example:** "Along Came Polly" has so much story before Ben Stiller dates Jennifer Aniston (the promise of the premise) that it feels slow.

**Application:** Get to the premise as quickly as possible.

### Additional Rules from Chapter Six

- **Too Much Marzipan** - Overdoing a good thing
- **Black Vet** - The cliche of giving characters quirks that feel forced
- **Watch Out for That Glacier!** - Problems that develop too slowly
- **Covenant of the Arc** - Every character must change (have an arc)

**Sources:**
- [Kelworth Files - Chapter Six Summary](https://kelworthfiles.wordpress.com/2011/02/19/blog-the-cat-chapter-six-common-sense-rules/)
- [Mythcreants - Blake Snyder's Rules](https://mythcreants.com/blog/how-useful-are-blake-snyders-rules-of-storytelling-part-1/)
- [Save the Cat - Glossary](https://savethecat.com/glossary)

---

## Part 7: Key Concepts and Terminology

### Whiff of Death

**Definition:** The added extra bonus in the All Is Lost beat where something dies, actually or metaphorically.

**Location:** Around page 75

**Forms:**
- Literal death of a character (mentor, friend)
- Death of a dream
- Death of a relationship
- Death of innocence
- Thought of death or near-death moment
- Death of identity ("Who am I without this?")

**Purpose:** Marks the end of the world as is, beginning of new world hero will create.

### Six Things That Need Fixing

**Definition:** List of things missing or needing change in the hero's life, established in Act One.

**Components:**
- Character flaws
- Basic needs
- Wishes
- Enemies and rivals
- Things missing from hero's life
- Problems to be solved

**Purpose:** Create "call-backs" that are satisfying when "paid off" later.

**Snyder's Note:** Six is an arbitrary number - could be more or less.

### The Transformation Machine

**Definition:** From "Save the Cat! Strikes Back" - the story itself is a machine that transforms the protagonist.

### Five-Point Finale

**Definition:** From "Save the Cat! Strikes Back" - the five beats within the Finale beat:

1. **Gathering the Team** - Assembling allies
2. **Executing the Plan** - Initial attempt
3. **High Tower Surprise** - Complication at the worst moment
4. **Dig Deep Down** - Hero finds inner strength
5. **Execution of New Plan** - Victory using what was learned

**Sources:**
- [Save the Cat - Glossary](https://savethecat.com/glossary)
- [Indie Cinema Academy](https://indiecinemaacademy.com/save-the-cat-screenwriting-book-chapter-four-summary/)
- [StudioBinder](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/what-is-save-the-cat-screenwriting/)

---

## Part 8: Beat Percentages and Calculations

### Standard 110-Page Screenplay

| Beat | Page | Percentage |
|------|------|------------|
| Opening Image | 1 | 1% |
| Theme Stated | 5 | 5% |
| Set-Up | 1-10 | 1-10% |
| Catalyst | 12 | 10-12% |
| Debate | 12-25 | 12-25% |
| Break into Two | 25 | 25% |
| B Story | 30 | 30% |
| Fun and Games | 30-55 | 30-55% |
| Midpoint | 55 | 50% |
| Bad Guys Close In | 55-75 | 55-75% |
| All Is Lost | 75 | 75% |
| Dark Night | 75-85 | 75-85% |
| Break into Three | 85 | 85% |
| Finale | 85-110 | 85-99% |
| Final Image | 110 | 100% |

### Act Distribution

- **Act One:** 25% (Pages 1-25)
- **Act Two:** 50% (Pages 25-85)
- **Act Three:** 25% (Pages 85-110)

### Calculating for Different Lengths

**For Novels:**
- Take total word count
- Multiply by percentage
- Example: 80,000 words x .50 = Midpoint at 40,000 words

**For Scenes:**
- Take total scene count
- Multiply by percentage
- Example: 56 scenes x .50 = Midpoint at scene 28

**Online Tool:** [Beat Sheet Calculator](https://www.beatsheetcalculator.com/)

**Sources:**
- [Kindlepreneur](https://kindlepreneur.com/save-the-cat-beat-sheet/)
- [Reedsy Guide](https://blog.reedsy.com/guide/story-structure/save-the-cat-beat-sheet/)
- [Savannah Gilbo](https://www.savannahgilbo.com/blog/plotting-save-the-cat)

---

## Part 9: Film Examples and Analysis

### The Matrix (1999) - Superhero Genre

| Beat | Scene |
|------|-------|
| Opening Image | Blinking command line cursor |
| Theme Stated | Told he needs to "unplug" |
| Catalyst | Meeting Morpheus |
| Break into Two | Takes the red pill |
| Fun and Games | Learning martial arts in The Construct |
| Midpoint | Visits the Oracle |
| All Is Lost | Morpheus captured |
| Dark Night | Must choose to save Morpheus |
| Break into Three | Realizes he is The One |
| Final Image | Flying |

### The Hunger Games - Dude with a Problem

| Beat | Scene |
|------|-------|
| Opening Image | Katniss providing for family in District 12 |
| Catalyst | Prim's name drawn at the Reaping |
| Break into Two | Volunteering as tribute |
| Fun and Games | Training, interviews, the arena |
| Midpoint | Alliance with Rue |
| All Is Lost | Rue's death |
| Break into Three | New rules allow two winners |

### Jurassic Park - Monster in the House

| Beat | Scene |
|------|-------|
| Opening Image | Dinosaur attack in darkness |
| Theme Stated | Life finds a way |
| Fun and Games | Seeing the dinosaurs |
| Midpoint | T. rex escapes - false defeat |
| All Is Lost | Trapped with raptors |
| Final Image | Birds - dinosaurs evolved |

### Other Films Analyzed by Snyder

From "Save the Cat! Goes to the Movies":
- Alien, Fatal Attraction, Scream (Monster in the House)
- Bad News Bears, Saving Private Ryan (Golden Fleece)
- Freaky Friday, The Nutty Professor (Out of the Bottle)
- Die Hard, The Martian (Dude with a Problem)
- Being There, Forrest Gump (Fool Triumphant)
- All the President's Men, Blade Runner (Whydunit)
- M*A*S*H, Office Space (Institutionalized)
- Raging Bull, Gladiator (Superhero)

**Sources:**
- [Save the Cat - Beat Sheets](https://savethecat.com/beat-sheets)
- [StudioBinder](https://www.studiobinder.com/blog/save-the-cat-beat-sheet/)
- [Scrite - Beat Sheet with Examples](https://www.scrite.io/blake-snyders-save-the-cat-structure-beat-sheet/)

---

## Part 10: Criticisms and Limitations

### Common Criticisms

**1. Overly Formulaic**
- Critics argue Snyder's precise timing creates sameness in Hollywood films
- The Slate article "Save the Movie!" blamed the book for Hollywood's creative decline

**2. Commercialism Over Art**
- Methodology prioritizes commercial viability
- Some argue it stifles artistic expression

**3. Rigid Structure**
- Exact page numbers feel restrictive
- Not all stories fit the 15-beat mold

### Snyder's Response

Snyder distinguished between "structure" and "formula" in "Save the Cat! Strikes Back":
- Structure = principles that work
- Formula = mechanical application without understanding

### When to Break the Rules

- Art films and experimental work
- Very short or very long formats
- Non-western storytelling traditions
- Stories intentionally subverting expectations

### The Balanced View

"Save the Cat! is a useful blueprint that many winning films and novels follow. But the book's claims are wildly overstated and treated as truths about storytelling. Such claims help sell more copies, but they don't respect the more complex truth that there is no blueprint for how to write a book."

"Use Save the Cat as a map, not a cage. The best stories honor the structure without letting it overshadow character, emotion, and voice."

**Sources:**
- [Slate - Save the Movie](https://slate.com/culture/2013/07/hollywood-and-blake-snyders-screenwriting-book-save-the-cat.html)
- [Reedsy - Beat Sheet Guide](https://blog.reedsy.com/guide/story-structure/save-the-cat-beat-sheet/)
- [The Narrative Morpheus](https://www.thenarrativecraft.com/post/the-save-the-cat-beat-sheet-a-cultural-anthropological-approach)

---

## Part 11: Application to Novels

### Save the Cat! Writes a Novel

Jessica Brody adapted Snyder's methodology for novelists in "Save the Cat! Writes a Novel: The Last Book on Novel Writing You'll Ever Need."

### Key Adaptations

**Word Count Instead of Page Numbers:**
- Opening Image: 0-1% of word count
- Catalyst: 10%
- Break into Two: 20-25%
- Midpoint: 50%
- All Is Lost: 75%
- Final Image: 100%

**Scene Count Method:**
- Calculate total scenes
- Apply percentages to scene numbers
- More flexible for varying novel lengths

### Novel-Specific Considerations

**Longer Development:**
- Novels can have more elaborate Set-Ups
- More room for internal processing
- Dark Night of Soul can be deeply explored

**Multiple POV:**
- Each POV character can have their own beat sheet
- Interweave multiple transformation arcs

**Subplots:**
- B Story can be more complex
- Multiple B Stories possible

**Sources:**
- [Jessica Brody - Save the Cat Beat Sheet](https://www.jessicabrody.com/2020/11/how-to-write-your-novel-using-the-save-the-cat-beat-sheet/)
- [Writing Mastery - What is a Beat Sheet](https://www.writingmastery.com/blog/what-is-a-beat-sheet)
- [Savannah Gilbo - Plotting Guide](https://www.savannahgilbo.com/blog/plotting-save-the-cat)

---

## Part 12: Summary Checklists

### Beat Sheet Checklist

- [ ] Opening Image shows the "before" state
- [ ] Theme Stated around 5%
- [ ] Set-Up introduces all major characters and Six Things That Need Fixing
- [ ] Catalyst disrupts the status quo around 10-12%
- [ ] Debate shows reluctance to change
- [ ] Break into Two at 25% - clear choice made
- [ ] B Story introduced around 30%
- [ ] Fun and Games delivers on premise (30-55%)
- [ ] Midpoint at 50% - false victory or defeat, stakes raised
- [ ] Bad Guys Close In - external and internal pressure mounts
- [ ] All Is Lost at 75% with Whiff of Death
- [ ] Dark Night of Soul - hero processes and prepares to change
- [ ] Break into Three at 85% - A and B stories combine for solution
- [ ] Finale uses lessons learned, changes the world
- [ ] Final Image mirrors Opening Image showing transformation

### Genre Identification Checklist

- [ ] Is there a monster and a confined space? (Monster in the House)
- [ ] Is there a journey with a prize? (Golden Fleece)
- [ ] Is there a wish or magic element? (Out of the Bottle)
- [ ] Is there an innocent in sudden danger? (Dude with a Problem)
- [ ] Is there a life transition or passage? (Rites of Passage)
- [ ] Is there a relationship driving the plot? (Buddy Love)
- [ ] Is there a mystery about human nature? (Whydunit)
- [ ] Is there an underdog vs. establishment? (Fool Triumphant)
- [ ] Is there group vs. individual tension? (Institutionalized)
- [ ] Is there an extraordinary hero? (Superhero)

### Logline Checklist

- [ ] Contains irony
- [ ] Creates compelling mental picture
- [ ] Implies audience and cost
- [ ] Has adjective for hero
- [ ] Has adjective for villain/antagonist
- [ ] Presents compelling, relatable goal
- [ ] Paired with killer title

### Board Method Checklist

- [ ] Four rows set up correctly
- [ ] 40 cards (approximately)
- [ ] Each card has scene description
- [ ] Each card has conflict symbol (><)
- [ ] Each card has emotional change (+/-)
- [ ] Color-coded by character/storyline/theme
- [ ] Can see entire story at a glance

## Referência: references/squad/research/donald-miller-storybrand-research.md

# Deep Research: Donald Miller - StoryBrand SB7 Framework

**Research Date:** 2026-01-30
**Researcher:** AIOX Squad Architect
**Purpose:** Create AI agent to help businesses clarify their message using the StoryBrand framework
**Domain:** Storytelling / Brand Messaging / Marketing

---

## Executive Summary

Donald Miller is a New York Times bestselling author and CEO of StoryBrand, Business Made Simple, and Coach Builder. His StoryBrand framework has helped over half a million business leaders clarify their messaging using the universal principles of storytelling. The core insight: **"Your customer is the hero, not your brand."**

The SB7 (StoryBrand 7-Part) Framework takes the structure of classic storytelling and applies it to brand messaging. The methodology transforms confusing marketing messages into clear, compelling narratives that drive customer action. The central mantra: **"If you confuse, you'll lose."**

This research provides complete documentation of the SB7 framework, including all seven elements, the three levels of problems, guide positioning (empathy + authority), plan types, CTAs, stakes, success outcomes, and practical implementation tools like the BrandScript and One-Liner.

---

## 1. DONALD MILLER - BACKGROUND & AUTHORITY

### Biography

**Full Name:** Donald Miller
**Born:** August 12, 1971, Houston, Texas
**Current Residence:** Nashville, Tennessee (with wife Elizabeth and daughter Emmeline)
**Role:** CEO of StoryBrand, Business Made Simple, and Coach Builder

### Professional Journey

- **1999:** Published first work "Prayer and the Art of Volkswagen Maintenance"
- **2003:** Breakthrough with "Blue Like Jazz" - memoir about faith
- **2008:** Founded The Mentoring Project (charity for fatherless boys)
- **2014:** Founded StoryBrand - company dedicated to helping businesses grow through effective marketing
- **2017:** Published "Building a StoryBrand" - introduced the SB7 Framework
- **2024:** Published "Building a StoryBrand 2.0" - updated for digital landscape

### Notable Works

1. **Building a StoryBrand** (2017) - New York Times & Wall Street Journal Bestseller
2. **Marketing Made Simple** (with J.J. Peterson)
3. **Business Made Simple** - 60 Days to Master Leadership, Sales, Marketing
4. **How to Grow Your Small Business**
5. **Blue Like Jazz** - Memoir
6. **A Million Miles in a Thousand Years**

### Authority Markers (PRIMARY)

- Over 500,000 business leaders trained in StoryBrand methodology
- Consulted with Fortune 500 brands: Berkshire Hathaway, Intel, Chick-fil-A, Steelcase
- Host of Business Made Simple podcast
- Dr. J.J. Peterson (StoryBrand co-author) wrote doctoral dissertation validating the framework's effectiveness
- StoryBrand Certified Guide program with hundreds of certified professionals worldwide

**Source:** [Donald Miller Official Bio](https://www.donaldmiller.com/), [Leaders.com Profile](https://leaders.com/rankings/person/donald-miller/)

---

## 2. THE SB7 FRAMEWORK - COMPLETE ANATOMY

### Core Philosophy (PRIMARY)

> "Your business is not the hero of your brand story. Your customer is."
> — Donald Miller, Building a StoryBrand

> "Pretty websites don't sell things. Words sell things. And if we haven't clarified our message, our customers won't listen."
> — Donald Miller

> "If you confuse, you'll lose."
> — StoryBrand Mantra

### The 7 Principles of StoryBrand (PRIMARY)

| # | Principle | Explanation |
|---|-----------|-------------|
| 1 | The customer is the hero, not your brand | Position customer as protagonist |
| 2 | Companies sell solutions to external problems, but customers buy solutions to internal problems | Address emotional needs, not just practical |
| 3 | Customers aren't looking for another hero; they're looking for a guide | Be Yoda, not Luke |
| 4 | Customers trust a guide who has a plan | Reduce perceived risk with clear steps |
| 5 | Customers do not take action unless challenged | Include clear calls to action |
| 6 | Every human being is trying to avoid a tragic ending | Communicate stakes |
| 7 | Never assume people understand how your brand can change their lives | Tell them explicitly |

### The 7 Elements of the SB7 Formula (PRIMARY)

```
1. CHARACTER (Hero) → The customer wants something
2. PROBLEM → They encounter a problem stopping them
3. GUIDE → Your brand steps in as the guide
4. PLAN → The guide provides a plan
5. CALL TO ACTION → The guide calls them to act
6. FAILURE (Stakes) → What they stand to lose
7. SUCCESS → What they might gain
```

### Story Structure Visualization

```
A CHARACTER who wants something
    ↓
encounters a PROBLEM before they can get it
    ↓
At the peak of their despair, a GUIDE steps into their lives
    ↓
gives them a PLAN
    ↓
and CALLS THEM TO ACTION
    ↓
That action helps them avoid FAILURE
    ↓
and ends in a SUCCESS
```

**Source:** [Building a StoryBrand Book](https://www.amazon.com/Building-StoryBrand-Clarify-Message-Customers/dp/0718033329), [Shortform Summary](https://www.shortform.com/blog/donald-miller-building-a-storybrand/)

---

## 3. ELEMENT 1: CHARACTER (THE HERO)

### Definition

The Character is your customer - they are the hero of the story, not your brand. The first step is to clearly define what your customer WANTS.

### Key Rules (PRIMARY)

1. **Identify ONE clear want** - Not multiple, not vague
2. **Make it specific and tangible** - Something they can visualize
3. **Create a "story gap"** - Distance between where they are and where they want to be
4. **Focus on survival-related desires** - Things connected to thriving

### Customer Wants Categories

| Category | Examples |
|----------|----------|
| **Conserve resources** | Save time, save money, reduce effort |
| **Social status** | Be respected, admired, connected |
| **Accumulate resources** | Make money, gain skills, acquire |
| **Meaning & purpose** | Contribute, make a difference |
| **Safety** | Feel secure, protected, stable |
| **Love & belonging** | Connection, community, acceptance |

### Character Definition Template

```yaml
character:
  who: "[Specific description of your ideal customer]"
  wants: "[One clear, specific desire]"
  current_state: "[Where they are now]"
  desired_state: "[Where they want to be]"
  story_gap: "[The distance between current and desired]"
```

### Examples

**Financial Advisor:**
- Who: Professionals in their 40s-50s approaching retirement
- Wants: Financial security and freedom in retirement
- Story Gap: Uncertainty about whether they have enough saved

**Meal Delivery Service:**
- Who: Busy moms who want their families to eat healthier
- Wants: Healthy meals without the time investment
- Story Gap: No time to shop and cook healthy meals

**Source:** [StoryBrand Framework Guide](https://www.gravityglobal.com/blog/complete-guide-storybrand-framework), [ROI Online](https://www.roionline.com/post/storybrand-7-part-framework-guide-your-brand-story-begins-here)

---

## 4. ELEMENT 2: PROBLEM (Three Levels)

### The Three Levels of Problems (PRIMARY)

This is one of Miller's most important insights: **"Companies tend to sell solutions to external problems, but customers buy solutions to internal problems."**

#### Level 1: EXTERNAL PROBLEM

**Definition:** The tangible, surface-level problem that can be pointed to.

**Characteristics:**
- Observable and measurable
- What you might think you're solving
- Often the "obvious" problem
- Usually tied to a physical or practical need

**Examples:**
| Business | External Problem |
|----------|------------------|
| Real Estate | Need more space for a growing family |
| IT Services | Computer systems are slow and outdated |
| Fitness Coach | Want to lose 20 pounds |
| Financial Advisor | Don't have a retirement plan |

#### Level 2: INTERNAL PROBLEM

**Definition:** The emotional challenges and feelings the external problem creates.

**Characteristics:**
- How the external problem makes them FEEL
- Often frustration, fear, confusion, overwhelm, doubt
- This is what they ACTUALLY buy solutions for
- The emotional weight of the problem

**Examples:**
| Business | Internal Problem |
|----------|------------------|
| Real Estate | Stressed about change, doubting they can find what they want |
| IT Services | Frustrated, embarrassed by system failures in front of clients |
| Fitness Coach | Feel insecure, ashamed of how they look |
| Financial Advisor | Anxious about the future, worried they'll be a burden |

#### Level 3: PHILOSOPHICAL PROBLEM

**Definition:** Why this matters in the grand scheme of things. The "ought" or "should" statement.

**Characteristics:**
- Connects to larger sense of right/wrong
- Why the story MATTERS
- Often starts with "People should..." or "It's not right that..."
- Elevates the problem to something meaningful

**Examples:**
| Business | Philosophical Problem |
|----------|----------------------|
| Real Estate | Everyone deserves a home that fits their life |
| IT Services | Technology should make life easier, not harder |
| Fitness Coach | People deserve to feel confident in their own bodies |
| Financial Advisor | You should be able to enjoy what you've worked for |

### The Villain (Problem Personified)

In story terms, the PROBLEM often takes the form of a VILLAIN. Miller advises giving the problem a "face" or personifying it.

**Good Villains:**
- Are a root source of the problem
- Are relatable to your audience
- Have a single, clear identity
- Are real (not abstract concepts)

**Example Villains:**
- High fees (for financial services)
- Complexity (for technology)
- Time thieves (for productivity tools)
- Wasted money (for efficiency solutions)

### Problem Section Template

```yaml
problem:
  villain: "[Personified root cause of problem]"
  external: "[Tangible, observable problem]"
  internal: "[How it makes them feel]"
  philosophical: "[Why it matters - the 'ought' statement]"
```

**Source:** [Capitaltec StoryBrand Guide](https://www.capitaltec.com/en-blog-posts/building-a-storybrand-marketing-strategy), [Innate Marketing Genius](https://www.innatemarketinggenius.com/storybrand-framework/)

---

## 5. ELEMENT 3: GUIDE (Empathy + Authority)

### The Core Concept (PRIMARY)

> "Customers aren't looking for another hero; they're looking for a guide."
> — Donald Miller

Your brand is NOT the hero. Your brand is the GUIDE - like Yoda to Luke Skywalker, or Gandalf to Frodo.

### The Two Characteristics of an Effective Guide

A guide MUST demonstrate BOTH:

#### 1. EMPATHY

**Definition:** A deep understanding of the customer's problem and a sincere desire to help.

**What Empathy IS:**
- Understanding their struggles
- Acknowledging their feelings
- Showing you "get it"
- Demonstrating you've been there or helped others there

**What Empathy IS NOT:**
- Pity
- Talking about yourself
- Dismissing their concerns
- Generic "we care" statements

**Empathy Statement Formula:**
> "We understand what it's like to [specific struggle]."

**Examples:**
| Business | Empathy Statement |
|----------|-------------------|
| Financial Advisor | "We know how overwhelming it can feel to plan for retirement when there are so many unknowns" |
| IT Services | "We understand the frustration of technology that's supposed to help but ends up wasting your time" |
| Fitness Coach | "We know what it's like to look in the mirror and not recognize yourself anymore" |

#### 2. AUTHORITY

**Definition:** Demonstrated competence that proves you can solve the problem.

**Ways to Demonstrate Authority:**
| Type | Examples |
|------|----------|
| **Testimonials** | Customer success stories, video testimonials |
| **Statistics** | "Helped 10,000+ businesses", "97% success rate" |
| **Awards** | Industry recognition, certifications |
| **Logos** | Client logos, media appearances |
| **Experience** | Years in business, projects completed |
| **Credentials** | Certifications, degrees, partnerships |

**Authority Statement Formula:**
> "[Credential/proof] that demonstrates [competence relevant to their problem]."

**Examples:**
| Business | Authority Statement |
|----------|---------------------|
| Financial Advisor | "For 20 years, we've helped over 500 families retire with confidence" |
| IT Services | "Certified by Microsoft, we've completed 1,000+ successful implementations" |
| Fitness Coach | "Certified trainer who's helped 2,000 clients lose a combined 50,000 pounds" |

### Balance is Critical (PRIMARY)

**Too much empathy, no authority:** "You seem nice, but can you actually help me?"
**Too much authority, no empathy:** "You're impressive, but do you understand my situation?"

The guide must show BOTH to earn trust.

### Guide Section Template

```yaml
guide:
  empathy:
    statement: "[We understand what it's like to...]"
    proof: "[How you demonstrate understanding]"
  authority:
    type: "[testimonials, statistics, awards, experience]"
    proof: "[Specific credentials or evidence]"
    statement: "[Authority positioning statement]"
```

**Source:** [Studio Founded](https://www.studiofounded.com/brand-materials/compelling-brand-stories-story-brand-framework), [Creativeo](https://www.creativeo.co/post/yes-your-customers-are-looking-for-a-guide-not-a-hero)

---

## 6. ELEMENT 4: PLAN (Process + Agreement)

### Why Plans Matter (PRIMARY)

> "Customers trust a guide who has a plan."
> — StoryBrand Principle Four

Plans serve two critical functions:
1. **Clarify HOW** to do business with you
2. **Reduce RISK** in the customer's mind

Without a plan, customers face hesitation: "What if it doesn't work?", "Will it be too complicated?", "Is it worth the risk?"

### Two Types of Plans

#### TYPE 1: PROCESS PLAN

**Purpose:** Clarify the steps to do business with you or use your product.

**Rules:**
- Keep it to **3-6 steps** (3 is ideal)
- Simple, clear language
- Show the path to success
- If more than 6 steps, combine into phases

**Pre-Purchase Process Plan Template:**
```
1. [First step - usually engagement/discovery]
2. [Second step - usually customization/planning]
3. [Third step - usually execution/results]
```

**Examples:**

**Financial Advisor:**
1. Schedule a discovery call
2. We create your personalized retirement plan
3. Watch your wealth grow with confidence

**IT Services:**
1. Book your free tech audit
2. We implement your custom solution
3. Enjoy stress-free technology

**Real Estate:**
1. Tell us about your dream home
2. We show you only homes that match
3. Move into your perfect home

**Post-Purchase Process Plan Template:**
```
1. [Onboarding/setup step]
2. [Integration/learning step]
3. [Success/results step]
```

#### TYPE 2: AGREEMENT PLAN

**Purpose:** Alleviate fears and reduce perceived risk.

**Rules:**
- Address the customer's INTERNAL fears
- Make promises that overcome objections
- Can be displayed publicly or used in sales conversations
- Focus on risk reduction and shared values

**Agreement Plan Types:**

| Type | Purpose | Example |
|------|---------|---------|
| **Risk Reduction** | Reduce fear of bad outcome | "Satisfaction guaranteed or your money back" |
| **Quality Assurance** | Prove competence | "Every product tested against 50-point checklist" |
| **Shared Values** | Build trust through alignment | "We only recommend what we'd use ourselves" |

**Examples:**

**Financial Advisor Agreement Plan:**
- We always act as a fiduciary (your interests first)
- No hidden fees, ever
- You'll understand every recommendation we make
- 24/7 access to your accounts and advisors

**IT Services Agreement Plan:**
- Same-day response guarantee
- No geek-speak - plain English only
- Fixed pricing - no surprise bills
- We don't leave until it works

### Naming Your Plan (PRIMARY)

> "Give your plan a name to increase its perceived value."
> — Donald Miller

**Good Plan Names:**
- "The Quick Start Plan"
- "The Peace of Mind Package"
- "The Easy Installation Process"
- "Our Customer Success Promise"

### Plan Section Template

```yaml
plan:
  process:
    name: "[Name of your process plan]"
    steps:
      - step_1: "[First step]"
      - step_2: "[Second step]"
      - step_3: "[Third step]"
  agreement:
    name: "[Name of your agreement/guarantee]"
    promises:
      - "[Promise 1]"
      - "[Promise 2]"
      - "[Promise 3]"
```

**Source:** [ClearBrand](https://clearbrand.com/storybrand-agreement-plan/), [Results & Co](https://resultsandco.com.au/blog/what-is-the-process-plan-of-storybrand/), [ROI Online](https://www.roionline.com/post/how-to-make-the-purchase-decision-easier-with-the-storybrand-agreement-plan)

---

## 7. ELEMENT 5: CALL TO ACTION (Direct + Transitional)

### Why CTAs Matter (PRIMARY)

> "Customers do not take action unless they are challenged to take action."
> — StoryBrand Principle Five

Most businesses are too passive with their CTAs. Miller uses the analogy: **"Marry me or date me"** - you need BOTH types.

### Two Types of CTAs

#### TYPE 1: DIRECT CTA ("Marry Me")

**Purpose:** Ask for the sale directly.

**Characteristics:**
- Clear, bold, specific
- Single action
- Visible and prominent
- Not hidden or apologetic

**Direct CTA Examples:**
| Weak | Strong |
|------|--------|
| "Contact us" | "Schedule Your Free Consultation" |
| "Learn more" | "Get Started Today" |
| "Submit" | "Claim Your Spot Now" |
| "Click here" | "Download Your Free Guide" |

**Rules for Direct CTAs:**
1. Use action verbs: "Get", "Start", "Schedule", "Claim"
2. Be specific about what happens next
3. Make it visually prominent (button, contrasting color)
4. Repeat it multiple times on the page
5. Don't be afraid to be bold

#### TYPE 2: TRANSITIONAL CTA ("Date Me")

**Purpose:** Capture leads who aren't ready to buy yet.

**Characteristics:**
- Low commitment
- Offers value in exchange for contact info
- Builds trust over time
- Positions you as the guide

**Transitional CTA Types:**
| Type | Example |
|------|---------|
| **PDF/Guide** | "Download our free retirement planning checklist" |
| **Video Series** | "Watch our 3-part video series on [topic]" |
| **Webinar** | "Register for our free masterclass" |
| **Quiz/Assessment** | "Take our 5-minute assessment" |
| **Free Trial** | "Start your 14-day free trial" |
| **Checklist** | "Get our complete [process] checklist" |

**The Key Insight:**
> "The transitional CTA positions yourself as the guide and shows you know your stuff."
> — Donald Miller

### CTA Placement Rules (PRIMARY)

1. **Header:** Direct CTA button (always visible)
2. **Throughout page:** Direct CTA repeated
3. **Sidebar/Footer:** Transitional CTA
4. **Exit intent:** Transitional CTA popup
5. **Email signature:** Transitional CTA

### CTA Section Template

```yaml
calls_to_action:
  direct:
    primary: "[Main CTA button text]"
    variations:
      - "[Alternative CTA 1]"
      - "[Alternative CTA 2]"
  transitional:
    offer: "[What you're giving away]"
    cta_text: "[Button text for lead magnet]"
    format: "[PDF, video, webinar, quiz, etc.]"
```

**Source:** [Results & Co](https://resultsandco.com.au/blog/how-to-transitional-call-to-action-lead-magnet), [Agency Boon](https://www.agencyboon.com/blog/5-things-your-storybrand-website-should-include/)

---

## 8. ELEMENT 6: FAILURE (Stakes)

### Why Stakes Matter (PRIMARY)

> "Every human being is trying to avoid a tragic ending."
> — StoryBrand Principle Six

> "People hate losing something more than they like winning something."
> — Loss Aversion Principle

Without stakes, there's no urgency. Without urgency, there's no action.

### The Psychology of Stakes

**Loss Aversion:** People are more motivated by what they might LOSE than what they might GAIN. This is why the failure element is essential - it creates urgency.

**The Story Question:** In every scene of a movie, the audience must know: "What's at stake for the hero?" Your marketing must answer the same question.

### Types of Stakes

| Category | Questions to Ask |
|----------|------------------|
| **Financial Loss** | What money could they lose or miss out on? |
| **Time Wasted** | What time will they never get back? |
| **Opportunity Cost** | What opportunities will they miss? |
| **Quality of Life** | How will their daily life suffer? |
| **Relationships** | How might their relationships be affected? |
| **Health** | What health consequences might occur? |
| **Status** | How might their reputation or status suffer? |

### Examples of Stakes

**Financial Advisor:**
- Without a plan, you might outlive your money
- You could miss the retirement you've worked your whole life for
- Your family might face financial stress when you're gone

**IT Services:**
- System failure could cost you thousands in lost productivity
- A data breach could destroy customer trust
- You'll keep wasting time on problems that should be solved

**Fitness Coach:**
- Your energy will continue to decline
- You'll miss out on activities with your kids
- Health problems will only get worse

### The Balance: Stakes Without Fear-Mongering (PRIMARY)

**DO:**
- Mention stakes 2-3 times in your messaging
- Make them real and relevant
- Connect to customer's actual fears
- Use to create urgency, not panic

**DON'T:**
- Overdo it (not every sentence)
- Use scare tactics
- Make exaggerated claims
- Focus only on negative

### Stakes Section Template

```yaml
failure:
  without_solution:
    - "[What bad thing will happen/continue]"
    - "[What they'll miss out on]"
    - "[How their situation will worsen]"
  emotional_impact: "[How failure will make them feel]"
  urgency_creator: "[Why they should act now]"
```

**Source:** [Bluleadz](https://www.bluleadz.com/blog/storybrand-framework-and-brandscript), [Chatterbuzz](https://www.chatterbuzzmedia.com/blog/storybrand-brand-message-guide/)

---

## 9. ELEMENT 7: SUCCESS (Transformation)

### Why Success Matters (PRIMARY)

> "Never assume people understand how your brand can change their lives. Tell them."
> — StoryBrand Principle Seven

The success element paints a picture of what life looks like AFTER the customer uses your product/service. It's the "happy ending" of their story.

### The Three Dimensions of Success

#### 1. EXTERNAL SUCCESS

**Definition:** The tangible, observable outcomes.

**Questions:**
- What measurable results will they achieve?
- What will change in their external world?
- What will others see?

#### 2. INTERNAL SUCCESS

**Definition:** How they will FEEL after success.

**Questions:**
- What emotions will they experience?
- What internal struggles will be resolved?
- How will their self-perception change?

#### 3. ASPIRATIONAL IDENTITY

**Definition:** Who they will BECOME.

> "The key questions brands should ask are: Who does our customer want to become? What kind of person do they want to be? What is their aspirational identity?"
> — Donald Miller

**Aspirational Identity Examples:**
| From | To |
|------|-----|
| Overwhelmed beginner | Confident expert |
| Stressed executive | Peaceful leader |
| Struggling business owner | Successful entrepreneur |
| Insecure | Confident |
| Mediocre | Elite |
| Amateur | Professional |

### The Identity Transformation (PRIMARY)

> "Identity transformation is the most important part of the framework."
> — Donald Miller

This is the complete arc: Who is your customer NOW vs. Who will they BECOME?

**Example - Luke Skywalker:**
- Before: Lonely, insecure farm boy
- After: Confident Jedi Knight

**Example - Financial Advisor Client:**
- Before: Anxious person uncertain about retirement
- After: Confident retiree enjoying financial freedom

**Example - Fitness Coaching Client:**
- Before: Someone who avoids mirrors
- After: Someone who feels proud in their own skin

### Success Visualization

**Before/After Framework:**
```
BEFORE: [Current state - frustrated person looking at problem]
AFTER: [Future state - happy person experiencing success]
```

Use images, testimonials, and vivid descriptions to make success REAL and TANGIBLE.

### Success Section Template

```yaml
success:
  external:
    - "[Tangible outcome 1]"
    - "[Tangible outcome 2]"
    - "[Tangible outcome 3]"
  internal:
    - "[How they'll feel 1]"
    - "[How they'll feel 2]"
  identity_transformation:
    before: "[Who they are now]"
    after: "[Who they'll become]"
  aspirational_identity: "[The identity they aspire to]"
```

**Source:** [Creativeo](https://www.creativeo.co/post/how-to-nail-the-identity-transformation-piece-of-your-storybrand-brandscript), [Reading Graphics](https://readingraphics.com/book-summary-building-a-storybrand/)

---

## 10. THE BRANDSCRIPT - COMPLETE TEMPLATE

### What is a BrandScript? (PRIMARY)

The BrandScript is the completed document that captures all seven elements of your brand's story. It serves as:
- The foundation for ALL marketing copy
- A reference for consistent messaging
- A training tool for your team
- A framework for every touchpoint

### BrandScript Access

**Free Tool:** mystorybrand.com
**Physical Templates:** Available at storybrand.com/downloads

### Complete BrandScript Template

```
At [COMPANY NAME] we know that you want to be a [IDENTITY TRANSFORMATION].

In order to do that, you need [CHARACTER WANT].

The problem is [EXTERNAL PROBLEM], which makes you feel [INTERNAL PROBLEM].

We believe [PHILOSOPHICAL PROBLEM].

We understand [EMPATHY], which is why we [AUTHORITY].

Here's how we do it:
1. [PLAN STEP 1]
2. [PLAN STEP 2]
3. [PLAN STEP 3]

So, [DIRECT CTA].

And in the meantime, [TRANSITIONAL CTA].

So you can stop [FAILURE] and instead [SUCCESS].
```

### BrandScript Example: Financial Advisor

```
At Smith Financial we know that you want to be a confident retiree who
never worries about money.

In order to do that, you need a clear, personalized retirement plan.

The problem is you don't know if you're on track, which makes you feel
anxious and overwhelmed about the future.

We believe everyone deserves to enjoy the retirement they've worked
their whole life for.

We understand how scary it is to think about running out of money,
which is why for 20 years we've helped over 500 families retire
with confidence.

Here's how we do it:
1. Schedule your free retirement clarity session
2. We create your personalized retirement roadmap
3. You enjoy retirement knowing you're on track

So, schedule your free session today.

And in the meantime, download our "5 Questions to Ask Before You Retire"
checklist.

So you can stop worrying about whether you'll have enough and instead
enjoy the retirement you deserve.
```

### BrandScript Application Points

Once complete, apply your BrandScript to:
- Website copy (especially homepage)
- Email marketing sequences
- Social media profiles and posts
- Sales presentations
- Elevator pitches
- Advertising copy
- Employee training
- Onboarding materials

**Source:** [MyStoryBrand.com](https://www.mystorybrand.com/), [Agency Boon Template Guide](https://www.agencyboon.com/blog/storybrand-brandscript-template/)

---

## 11. THE ONE-LINER

### What is the One-Liner? (PRIMARY)

The One-Liner is a concise statement (typically 2-4 sentences) that answers: **"What do you do?"**

> "The StoryBrand one-liner is a single statement that you can use to help people realize why they need your product or service."
> — Donald Miller

### One-Liner Formula

```
[PROBLEM] + [SOLUTION] + [SUCCESS]
```

**Detailed:**
1. Talk about the PROBLEM
2. Give the SOLUTION
3. Ramp up the SUCCESS

### One-Liner Template

```
[Identify the problem your customer faces].
[Introduce your solution].
[Describe the success they'll experience].
```

### One-Liner Examples

**Financial Advisor:**
"Most people approaching retirement don't know if they'll have enough money. We create personalized retirement plans that give you confidence. So you can enjoy the retirement you've worked your whole life for."

**IT Services:**
"Technology problems cost businesses thousands in lost productivity. We provide reliable IT support with same-day response. So you can focus on your business, not your computers."

**Real Estate Agent:**
"Finding the right home in this market is overwhelming. We match you with homes that fit your life. So you can find your dream home without the stress."

### Where to Use Your One-Liner

| Location | How to Use |
|----------|------------|
| **Website footer** | Display prominently |
| **Email signature** | Include in every email |
| **Social media bios** | Perfect for limited space |
| **Business cards** | First thing they read |
| **Networking events** | Your verbal introduction |
| **About page** | Opening or closing statement |
| **Elevator pitch** | Memorize and deliver confidently |

**Source:** [Results & Co](https://resultsandco.com.au/blog/storybrand-one-liner-donald-miller), [Hughes Integrated](https://hughesintegrated.com/storybrand-one-liner-examples/)

---

## 12. WEBSITE WIREFRAME IMPLEMENTATION

### StoryBrand Website Structure (PRIMARY)

Donald Miller provides a specific wireframe for implementing StoryBrand on websites. The key sections:

### Section 1: HEADER (Hero Section)

**Purpose:** Tell customers EXACTLY what you do in 5 seconds.

**Must Include:**
1. **Headline:** Benefit-focused statement (what they GET)
2. **Subheadline:** Brief clarification
3. **Direct CTA button:** Primary action
4. **Success image:** Visual of the outcome

**Rules:**
- Above the fold (no scrolling)
- Clear, not clever
- One message, not multiple
- CTA button visually prominent

**Example:**
```
Headline: "Retire with Confidence"
Subheadline: "We create personalized retirement plans for busy professionals"
CTA Button: "Schedule Your Free Session"
Image: Happy couple enjoying retirement
```

### Section 2: STAKES

**Purpose:** Communicate what's at risk.

**Must Include:**
- 3-4 bullet points of what they stand to lose
- Emotional language
- Connection to their real fears

### Section 3: VALUE PROPOSITION

**Purpose:** Explain what you offer.

**Must Include:**
- List of benefits (not features)
- How their life improves
- What they get

### Section 4: GUIDE

**Purpose:** Establish empathy and authority.

**Must Include:**
- Brief empathy statement
- Authority elements (logos, testimonials, statistics)
- Photo of founder/team (humanizes)

### Section 5: PLAN

**Purpose:** Show the simple path forward.

**Must Include:**
- 3-step process
- Numbered, visual steps
- Clear action at each stage

### Section 6: EXPLANATORY PARAGRAPH

**Purpose:** Provide more detail for those who want it.

**Must Include:**
- Expanded version of your message
- More about your approach
- Additional proof points

### Section 7: VIDEO (Optional)

**Purpose:** Engage visual learners.

**Must Include:**
- Short (under 2 minutes)
- Follows StoryBrand framework
- Clear CTA at end

### Section 8: PRICING/OPTIONS

**Purpose:** Make it easy to choose.

**Must Include:**
- Clear pricing tiers (if applicable)
- Comparison of options
- CTA for each option

### Section 9: JUNK DRAWER

**Purpose:** Everything else (FAQs, additional info).

**Location:** Lower on page, for those who need more.

### Section 10: FOOTER

**Purpose:** Final CTA and contact info.

**Must Include:**
- Repeated Direct CTA
- Contact information
- One-liner

**Source:** [StoryBrand Website Wireframe PDF](https://s3.amazonaws.com/kajabi-storefronts-production/sites/143641/themes/2147635196/downloads/HDimoVwHSiit3PpC1sgb_Website_Wireframe_Template_-_StoryBrand.pdf), [Creativeo Guide](https://www.creativeo.co/post/storybrand-framework)

---

## 13. EMAIL & NURTURE SEQUENCES

### StoryBrand Email Strategy (PRIMARY)

#### Nurture Emails

**Purpose:** Build trust over time with leads who aren't ready to buy.

**Characteristics:**
- Regular (weekly or monthly)
- Valuable content
- Positions you as guide
- Not salesy
- Keeps you top of mind

**Structure:**
1. Acknowledge their problem
2. Provide valuable insight/tip
3. Brief mention of how you help
4. Soft CTA (read more, reply, etc.)

#### Sales Emails

**Purpose:** Convert leads to customers.

**Characteristics:**
- Bold and direct
- Clear CTA
- Creates urgency
- Focuses on stakes and success

**The Rule of 7:**
> "The human brain needs to see your brand message 7-8 times before it can be internalized, noticed, and taken action on."
> — StoryBrand

### Email Sequence Template

**After Lead Magnet Download:**

| Day | Email Type | Purpose |
|-----|------------|---------|
| Day 0 | Delivery | Deliver lead magnet |
| Day 2 | Value | Additional helpful content |
| Day 4 | Value | More insight on their problem |
| Day 6 | Story | Testimonial or case study |
| Day 8 | Value | Another helpful tip |
| Day 10 | Soft Pitch | Introduce your solution |
| Day 12 | Sales | Clear CTA to buy |
| Day 14 | Sales | Urgency + final pitch |

**Timing:** Send 2-3 days apart, 7 AM or 11 AM in recipient's timezone.

**Source:** [Digital Dynamo](https://digitaldynamollc.com/using-storybrand-framework-create-effective-nurture-sales-email-campaigns/), [StoryWorks Marketing](https://storyworks.marketing/storybrand-email-sequence/)

---

## 14. COMMON MISTAKES TO AVOID

### The 10 StoryBrand Pitfalls (PRIMARY)

| # | Mistake | Solution |
|---|---------|----------|
| 1 | Making your brand the hero | Always position customer as hero |
| 2 | Failing to clarify message | Use the 7-part framework |
| 3 | Weak/vague CTAs | Use bold, specific action language |
| 4 | Inconsistent messaging | Apply BrandScript everywhere |
| 5 | Overcomplicating the story | Stick to simple, clear language |
| 6 | Too many words | Edit ruthlessly |
| 7 | Addressing too many problems | Focus on ONE main problem |
| 8 | One-time exercise | Use as ongoing framework |
| 9 | Only positive messaging | Include stakes and failure |
| 10 | Confusing customer journey | Clear path at every step |

### Red Flags in Your Messaging

**Your message is confusing if:**
- You can't explain what you do in 5 seconds
- Customers ask "but what do you actually do?"
- Your team explains your business differently
- Your website visitors bounce quickly
- You're not getting referrals

### The Confusion Test

> "If your message fails what we call the 'grunt test' — meaning a caveman couldn't look at your website and immediately grunt what you offer — you're confusing customers."
> — Donald Miller

**Source:** [Well Dressed Walrus](https://welldressedwalrus.com/7-common-storybrand-mistakes-and-how-to-avoid-them/), [Business Builders](https://businessbldrs.com/common-mistakes-to-avoid-in-storybrand-marketing/)

---

## 15. CONSOLIDATED FRAMEWORK

### Principles (Summary)

1. **Customer = Hero** - Your customer is the protagonist
2. **Internal > External** - Sell to emotional needs
3. **Be the Guide** - Demonstrate empathy + authority
4. **Provide a Plan** - Reduce perceived risk
5. **Call to Action** - Be bold and direct
6. **Communicate Stakes** - What they'll lose
7. **Paint Success** - What they'll gain and become

### Complete Process (Step-by-Step)

```
STEP 1: Define Your Character
- Who is your customer?
- What do they WANT?

STEP 2: Identify the Problem (3 Levels)
- External: What's the tangible problem?
- Internal: How does it make them feel?
- Philosophical: Why does this matter?

STEP 3: Position Yourself as Guide
- Write your empathy statement
- Gather your authority elements

STEP 4: Create Your Plan
- Define 3-step process plan
- Define agreement plan (if applicable)
- Name your plans

STEP 5: Define Your CTAs
- Direct CTA: What action do you want?
- Transitional CTA: What lead magnet do you offer?

STEP 6: Articulate the Stakes
- What happens if they don't act?
- What will they lose?

STEP 7: Paint Success
- External success: What results will they see?
- Internal success: How will they feel?
- Identity transformation: Who will they become?

STEP 8: Complete Your BrandScript
- Fill out the complete template
- Review for clarity and consistency

STEP 9: Create Your One-Liner
- Problem + Solution + Success
- Memorize it

STEP 10: Apply to All Marketing
- Website (using wireframe)
- Email sequences
- Social media
- Sales materials
- Employee training
```

### Quality Checklist

- [ ] Customer is positioned as hero (not your brand)
- [ ] Problem is defined at all 3 levels
- [ ] Empathy statement demonstrates understanding
- [ ] Authority elements prove competence
- [ ] Plan is 3 steps or fewer
- [ ] Direct CTA is bold and specific
- [ ] Transitional CTA offers real value
- [ ] Stakes create appropriate urgency
- [ ] Success is vivid and specific
- [ ] Identity transformation is clear
- [ ] BrandScript is complete
- [ ] One-liner answers "what do you do?"
- [ ] Message passes the "grunt test"
- [ ] Consistent across all channels

---

# Joseph Campbell's Hero's Journey: Comprehensive Research Document

**Research Date:** 2026-01-30
**Subject:** Joseph Campbell - The Monomyth and Hero's Journey Framework
**Purpose:** Deep research for creating storytelling agent and task

---

## Table of Contents

1. [Biographical Overview](#1-biographical-overview)
2. [Primary Sources and Key Works](#2-primary-sources-and-key-works)
3. [The Monomyth: Theoretical Foundation](#3-the-monomyth-theoretical-foundation)
4. [The 17 Stages (Campbell's Original)](#4-the-17-stages-campbells-original)
5. [The 12 Stages (Vogler Adaptation)](#5-the-12-stages-vogler-adaptation)
6. [The Three Acts: Departure, Initiation, Return](#6-the-three-acts-departure-initiation-return)
7. [Character Archetypes](#7-character-archetypes)
8. [Detailed Stage Analysis](#8-detailed-stage-analysis)

10. [Campbell's Philosophy and Quotes](#10-campbells-philosophy-and-quotes)
11. [Practical Application for Writers](#11-practical-application-for-writers)
12. [Common Pitfalls and Anti-Patterns](#12-common-pitfalls-and-anti-patterns)
13. [Sources and Citations](#13-sources-and-citations)
